HARD RULES FOR THIS RUN
- This is an independent Grok-owned survey. Cite ONLY papers that have a card filename in this folder's compact summaries / relevance index. Do not cite parent-survey papers that have no card here (Amazon Ads MTA 2025 paper, LiDDA arXiv paper, MAL, CABB, etc. are parent-only). LinkedIn's 2025 engineering blog card is in-scope; the LinkedIn arXiv paper is not.
- Every Q1/Q2 claim and every recommendation bullet must cite a paper's full name from a card you were given.
- Q2 classification uses the rubric in D3_retention_attribution.md: TRUE = per-touch retention credit with causal/MTA decomposition; PARTIAL = item-level scores or surrogates without identified causal MTA; NOT = RL / LTR / bandit / policy optimization. RL-for-retention (RLUR, GFN4Retention, AURO, SEC, OCARM, MRet) is not automatically attribution; say whether each produces per-interaction credit.
- Related-notebook NLM answers over-classify GFN/MRet as TRUE. Cards plus the D3 rubric override related-notebook labels.
- Card "Q2 class" headers are NotebookLM guesses; if they conflict with the D3 rubric applied to the card's method description, re-classify and say so.
- Netflix cards are stubs (`nlm:failed`). Do not treat discovery one-liners as measured results.
- v1 credit must NOT be called incremental unless a cited card estimates that exact contrast on N7 (or an analogous delayed binary retention label). A deletion residual, Shapley value, RL advantage, or attention score is not automatically incremental lift.
- Where NotebookLM synthesis and a card disagree, prefer the card and say so.
- Use only numbers present in the files. If a number is missing, write "not reported in the card".
- Do not explore `../20260917_survey/` or parent `../read-papers/`.

BACKGROUND (also in README.md). A dating platform attributes a user's next-7-day retention (N7: active on day 7 after a reference day) to the user's most recent swipes with LAST-TOUCH attribution and trains a ranking model on those labels. Q1 = the latest industry attribution approach (production/industry-authored systems 2025-01 → 2026-09, plus newest work from the follow-list teams). Q2 = has anyone published attribution of retention / engagement / days-active to individual in-app interactions (recs, matches, messages, notifications, swipes)? Academic work counts iff it names a production dataset or deployment. A 2026-06-13 decision fixed: binary N7 target; v1 = interpretable removal-effect attributor; v2 = calibrated deep data-driven attribution with a survival head. The executive summary must confirm or revise that plan with evidence from the cards. Analogies: ad touchpoint → swipe or match; conversion → N7; channel → like / match / conversation / notification.

OUTPUT FORMAT. After the proof-of-reading section, output the three documents, each preceded by a marker line exactly as shown, in this order:

===== FILE: method-tracker.md =====
(Method Tracker File Template: Methodology Table over the methods in the cards — method, paper, year, venue, company, unit of credit, outcome, bias device, deployment evidence; then "Top Method Analysis" ranking the top 5 for the dating retention problem with a one-paragraph justification each. Compute a simple composite score from: production deployment with numbers (0–3), per-interaction credit (0–3), selection-bias device (0–2), transfer rating from the card (direct 2 / adaptable 1 / background 0). Show the score. Also keep the template's baseline-mention / variant / simplicity columns if you can fill them from the cards; otherwise mark "n/a in cards".)

===== FILE: literature-review.md =====
(Main Review File Template. Categories to use, in this order: 1. Retention credited to individual interactions — reciprocal / two-sided platforms; 2. Retention credited to individual interactions — feed recommenders (RL/flow credit, per-item long-term value, per-action uplift); 3. Notification incrementality; 4. Industry attribution systems 2025–2026 (experiment-calibrated, causal, LLM, multi-attribution); 5. Incrementality measurement and evaluation devices; 6. Long-term outcome estimation (surrogate index and variants). Every in-scope card gets an entry per the template with a link to its card as `read-papers/<filename>`. Put weakly relevant cards under References (Low Relevance). Every claim names its paper.)

===== FILE: executive-summary.md =====
(Executive Summary File Template. Key Questions = Q1 and Q2 verbatim from README.md. Main Findings must open with a direct ≤200-word answer to Q1 and a direct ≤200-word answer to Q2. The Q2 answer MUST include an explicit TRUE / PARTIAL / NOT table covering every Q2-tagged card (G4–G11, G13–G27 at minimum), using the D3 rubric. Most Fundamental Methods = top 5 from method-tracker.md. Approach Comparison table. Recommendations = a phased plan (v1 / v2 / v3) to replace last-touch for per-swipe N7 credit mapped onto swipe / match / conversation touchpoints: for each phase give sources, the exact credit formula or estimator for ONE swipe (and how match and conversation enter), the selection-bias device, handling of the daily recurring binary outcome, the validation anchor, cost (low / medium / high), and main risk; then "Against the 2026-06-13 plan" (keep / change, with reasons); then "Why not keep last-touch" with the strongest evidence from the cards; then an evaluation protocol without per-interaction ground truth. Cite every paper by full name as `"Author et al., full paper title, venue year"`. Length 2–3 pages.)

Word budget across the three files: at most 7000 words. Write in plain, active-voice sentences. No preamble before the proof-of-reading section and no closing recap after the last file. End with one line: `FILES READ: <n> of 16`.

Delegate each phase:

    84	## Notebook Lifecycle
    85	
    86	One notebook per topic slug. The notebook ID is stored in `notebooklm-state.md` (notebook ID + created date + overflow notebook IDs only — no Source Map; `queue.md` is the sole source of truth for per-paper NLM source IDs).
    87	
    88	**On resume:** Read `notebooklm-state.md` → call `notebook_get(notebook_id)` to verify the notebook still exists.
    89	
    90	**Source limit guard:** NotebookLM's source cap depends on the account tier. This account is **Google AI Pro**, which supports up to **300 sources per notebook**. Before every `source_add`, count the current sources in the active notebook via `notebook_get`. If count ≥ 290, create an overflow notebook titled `[topic-slug]-overflow-N` (where N increments from 1), record its ID in `notebooklm-state.md`, and switch all subsequent `source_add` calls to it. (The old threshold of 45 was written for the free tier — do not use it.)
    91	
    92	### `notebooklm-state.md` format
    93	
    94	```markdown
    95	# NotebookLM State — [topic-slug]
    96	
    97	notebook_id: <id>
    98	created: <date>
    99	overflow_notebooks:
   100	  - [topic-slug]-overflow-1: <id>
   101	```

   113	
   114	## Phase 0.5: Ensure README.md exists
   115	
   116	Runs whether or not the folder is fresh. Supersedes the Phase 1 Step 6 "Project Context" prompt. Full prompt and README.md template: see Phase 0.5 section in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md`.
   117	
   118	## Phase 1: Preparation and Search Strategy Setup
   119	
   120	1. Create the output folder and the `read-papers/` subfolder if they do not exist.
   121	2. Extract 3–6 core keywords from the topic.
   122	3. Decide the search strategy based on the criteria in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-priorities.md` (relevant venues by area, paper prioritization, method fundamentality weight).
   123	4. Once the search strategy is decided, immediately create `requirements.md` using the template in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-templates.md`.
   124	   This file must record the user-requested topic, extracted keywords, selected venues/blogs, search queries, and survey scope constraints.
   125	5. After creating the requirements file, show its contents to the user and get confirmation.
   126	   If the user requests revisions, reflect them and confirm again.
   127	   Only after user approval may you proceed to Phase 2.
   128	
   129	6. **Project Context (already collected in Phase 0.5):** Project Context is collected and persisted in `README.md` during Phase 0.5. Do not re-prompt here. In `requirements.md`, include a one-line reference instead of duplicating the section:
   130	
   131	   ```markdown
   132	   ## Project Context
   133	
   134	   See `./README.md` for the canonical Project Context. This file references it; do not duplicate.
   135	   ```
   136	
   137	   All downstream phases that refer to `## Project Context` should read it from `README.md`. The Project Context is the **north star** for the entire survey — it shapes which papers are included, what is extracted from each paper (Phase 3 Query 3), how findings are synthesized (Phase 4 Queries 6–7), and how the executive summary is framed (Phase 5).
   138	
   139	7. **Must Include / Project Context consistency check (mandatory):** Procedure in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § Phase 1 Must Include / Project Context Consistency Check. Only proceed to Phase 2 after the user has confirmed or revised the Must Include list following this check.
   140	
   141	## Phase 2: Collect Seed Papers
   142	
   143	Do **not** list all 200 papers at once. First collect 15–25 seed papers, then gradually expand the list in Phase 3 by following related works from each paper.
   144	
   145	For Step 0 (Awesome repo scan) and Step 1 (web search query patterns + engineering blog list): see `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-discovery.md`.
   146	
   147	### Step 2 (NLM-specific): NLM Research Discovery
   148	
   149	1. Create the topic notebook: `notebook_create(title="[topic-slug]")` → save the returned `notebook_id` to `notebooklm-state.md`.
   150	2. Call `research_start(notebook_id=<id>, query="<topic keywords>", source="web", mode="deep")` → record the returned `task_id`.
   151	3. Poll `research_status(notebook_id=<id>, task_id=<id>, compact=True)` until status = complete. Do NOT use `compact=False` during polling.
   152	4. Make one final `research_status` call with `compact=False` to review all hits.
   153	5. Select relevant papers → call `research_import(notebook_id=<id>, task_id=<id>, source_indices=[...])`.
   154	6. Record the returned source IDs for each imported paper.
   155	
   156	### Step 3: Deduplicate and Build queue.md
   157	
   158	Union all sources from Steps 0, 1, and 2. Deduplicate by title. Apply all priority and relevance rules from `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-priorities.md` verbatim.
   159	
   160	`queue.md` format (extends the standard format with an `nlm:` column): see `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § queue.md Format.
   161	
   162	After seed collection, assess whether the target number of papers (200) is appropriate for the topic. If 200 seems excessive, stop at Phase 2 and confirm the target paper count with the user. Proceed with later phases using the approved target count.
   163	
   164	Also create an empty `method-tracker.md` (template in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-templates.md`).
   165	
   166	## Phase 3: Per-Paper Analysis via NLM
   167	
   168	This is the token-saving core. Instead of reading PDFs into Claude's context, Claude fires structured queries per paper to NotebookLM.
   169	
   170	**Delegation model:** Process papers in batches of 3–5. Each batch is delegated to a fresh general-purpose subagent via the Agent tool to prevent context bloat. The brief to each subagent must include: `notebook_id`, the batch paper list with source IDs and URLs, and the output folder path. After the subagent writes the batch outputs to disk, a new subagent is spawned for the next batch.
   171	
   172	For each paper in the batch:
   173	
   174	### 3-A: URL Normalization
   175	
   176	Before ingesting, normalize the URL:
   177	
   178	- **arXiv abstract URL** (`arxiv.org/abs/<ID>` or `arxiv.org/abs/<ID>v<N>`): convert to PDF URL → `https://arxiv.org/pdf/<ID>.pdf`
   179	- **Paywalled publisher URL** (IEEE Xplore `ieeexplore.ieee.org`, ACM DL `dl.acm.org`, Springer `link.springer.com`, Elsevier `sciencedirect.com`): do **not** skip immediately. First search for a free version using the four-step search order in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-priorities.md` § Accessibility Principle.
   180	
   181	  If a free URL is found, replace the URL with the free version and continue ingestion normally.
   182	  Only if all four checks return nothing: mark as `nlm:failed:paywall` in queue.md, log the search attempts in the Skipped section as `"paywall — checked arxiv, SSRN, Semantic Scholar, author homepage; no free version found"`, and skip.
   183	
   184	### 3-B: Ingest into NLM
   185	
   186	- If `nlm` status is `pending`:
   187	  - **Overflow check first:** Count sources in the current active notebook via `notebook_get`. If ≥ 290, create an overflow notebook (see Notebook Lifecycle above) and switch to it.
   188	  - Call `source_add(notebook_id=<id>, source_type="url", url=<normalized_url>, wait=False)` → record the returned `source_id`.
   189	  - Poll `notebook_get(notebook_id)` and check the source's status field until it becomes `ready`. Timeout = 120s. If timeout or error: mark `nlm:failed:<reason>`, add to Skipped section, continue to next paper.
   190	  - On success: update the queue entry from `nlm:pending` to `nlm:<source_id>`.
   191	- If `nlm` status is already `<source_id>`: already ingested — skip directly to 3-C.
   192	
   193	### 3-C: Two-Query Structured Extraction
   194	
   195	Use **independent queries** — do NOT use `conversation_id` threading. This prevents answer contamination between questions.
   196	
   197	**Query 1** — scope with `source_ids=[<source_id>]`:
   198	
   199	> "For the paper in this source, provide all of the following clearly labeled:
   200	> (1) Core problem and key contribution
   201	> (2) Proposed method or architecture in detail
   202	> (3) Datasets used for evaluation and comparison baselines"
   203	
   204	**Query 2** — scope with `source_ids=[<source_id>]`:
   205	
   206	> "For the paper in this source, provide all of the following clearly labeled:
   207	> (1) Key quantitative results and improvements over baselines
   208	> (2) Limitations, failure modes, or negative results noted by the authors
   209	> (3) Top 5–7 most heavily cited prior works named in the related work or introduction"
   210	
   211	**Query 3 (project-specific)** — scope with `source_ids=[<source_id>]`:
   212	
   213	The batch subagent **dynamically formulates** this query from the `## Project Context` section in `README.md`. It must probe whether and how this paper's method/findings address the project's specific needs — referencing the concrete framing (e.g., label type, noise model, downstream goal, audience questions), not just repeating the topic generically.
   214	
   215	This query runs for **all** papers unconditionally. If the answer indicates the paper does not address the project need, that is a useful signal — it flags a retrieval-phase relevance mismatch, not a query design problem.
   216	
   217	**NLM refusal handling:** After receiving all three responses, scan for refusal language: phrases like "I cannot answer", "not mentioned in the source", "I don't have enough information", or similar. Replace any such section's content with: `Not specified in source.` Do not copy NLM refusal boilerplate into the markdown output.
   218	
   219	### 3-D: Assemble Markdown
   220	
   221	Assemble the three Q&A responses into the standard `read-papers/` markdown file.
   222	
   223	Follow the **filename rules** and **report template** from `~/.claude/skills/paper-reader/SKILL.md` exactly. Do not copy the template into this skill — read it at runtime and apply it.
   224	
   225	When in `read-papers/` mode (as opposed to standalone paper-reader use), leave the Reverse Citation Map section blank — it is filled in during Phase 3.7. The Project Relevance and Reverse Citation Map section formats are in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-templates.md` § Per-Paper Markdown Additions.
   226	
   227	After the standard template sections, add a **Project Relevance** section containing the structured Query 3 answer:
   228	
   229	```markdown
   230	## Project Relevance
   231	[Query 3 answer, structured by the subagent]
   232	```
   233	
   234	If the Query 3 answer indicates the paper does not meaningfully address the project's needs, prefix the section with: `**Low project relevance.** [reason]`. This makes it scannable during review and serves as a retroactive check on the retrieval phase.
   235	
   236	**For Priority 3–4 / Peripheral papers:** write a one-paragraph summary only — do not fill every section. Still include the Project Relevance section.
   237	
   238	### 3-E: Update Tracking
   239	
   240	- Move paper from "To Process" → "Done" in queue.md, including the `nlm:<source_id>` field.
   241	- Update `method-tracker.md` with any newly observed methods, baseline mentions, variant counts, and component counts.
   242	- After finishing the batch, report a progress summary to the lead agent:
   243	  ```text
   244	  [Batch complete] Processed: X | Skipped: Y | Remaining: Z | NLM sources in notebook: N
   245	  ```
   246	
   247	**Note on cross-paper baseline question:** The "is this paper's method used as a baseline by others?" question cannot be answered per-paper because it requires seeing the full notebook. This is addressed in Phase 4-A (Query 5).
   248	
   249	### Queue Depletion Strategy
   250	
   251	If the queue risks running out before reaching the target paper count, expand it following the Queue Depletion Strategy in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-discovery.md`. The harvest source for the NLM variant is the markdowns in `read-papers/` (Related Works and Introduction sections), not PDFs.
   252	
   253	## Phase 3.5: Finalize Method Tracker
   254	
   255	See `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § Phase 3.5.
   256	
   257	## Phase 3.7: Cross-Reference Mapping
   258	
   259	After Phase 3.5 is complete, you **must** run this step. Do **not** start Phase 4 until this step is complete.
   260	Full procedure and gating rule: see `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § Phase 3.7.
   261	
   262	**Execution: dispatch to Gemini CLI** (via `cli-review` skill) with all files in `read-papers/` as the file list. Gemini reads the full corpus in one shot and returns the citation map. Do not delegate to a Claude subagent — at 200 papers, the corpus exceeds Claude's practical context for this step.
   263	
   264	## Phase 4: Literature Review (NLM-First Hybrid Synthesis)
   265	
   266	### 4-A: NLM Generates Core Draft
   267	
   268	Query the full notebook with no `source_ids` filter (all papers). Use these 5 queries as building blocks for `literature-review.md`:
   269	
   270	1. "What are the dominant methodological approaches across all papers in this notebook? For each approach, summarize it and list representative papers."
   271	2. "What are the most common evaluation datasets and benchmarks used across these papers? Which papers use which datasets?"
   272	3. "What open problems or research gaps are identified most frequently across these papers?"
   273	4. "Which papers appear to be the most foundational — cited by or built upon by many others in this notebook? List them with brief explanations."
   274	5. "Is any paper's method used as a direct baseline by other papers in this notebook? Map method name → list of papers that use it as a baseline."
   275	
   276	**Queries 6–7 (project-specific synthesis)** — dynamically formulated by the Phase 4 subagent from the `## Project Context` section in `README.md`. The subagent reads the Project Context and writes 1–2 cross-paper queries (no `source_ids` filter) that synthesize the notebook's papers through the lens of the project's specific needs. Examples: "Which methods in this notebook are applicable when labels are [project-specific label type]?" or "What does the collective evidence say about [project-specific concern]?" These become additional building blocks for `literature-review.md`.
   277	
   278	### 4-B: Gemini Structures into literature-review.md
   279	
   280	**Execution: dispatch to Gemini CLI** (via `cli-review` skill). Pass the 5–7 NLM query responses from Phase 4-A, the Main Review File Template from `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-templates.md`, and the `## Project Context` from `README.md`. Gemini consolidates the NLM responses into a `literature-review.md` draft. Claude Lead reviews the draft, runs the conflict check (Phase 4-C), and finalizes. Claude does **not** re-read all individual per-paper markdowns at this step.
   281	
   282	### 4-C: Conflict Check
   283	
   284	Claude spot-checks NLM's cross-paper claims against the per-paper markdowns already written (read only the specific ones relevant to a claimed conflict). If a conflict is found: flag it, re-query NLM with a follow-up question, and resolve before finalizing.
   285	
   286	## Phase 5: Write Outputs and Evaluate Coverage
   287	
   288	Note: Start this phase only after Phase 3.7 is fully complete.
   289	
   290	Complete the four files using the templates in `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-templates.md`:
   291	
   292	#### File 1: `requirements.md`
   293	
   294	- Created in Phase 1, updated here.
   295	- Fill in the "Summary of Actual Search Results" section.
   296	- Record total number of papers, number of categories, and main findings.
   297	
   298	#### File 2: `literature-review.md`
   299	
   300	Full paper list and category-wise summary. Each paper entry should include a link to the individual file in `read-papers/`.
   301	
   302	#### File 3: `executive-summary.md`
   303	
   304	**Execution: dispatch to Gemini CLI** (via `cli-review` skill). Pass `literature-review.md`, `method-tracker.md`, `README.md` (for Project Context), and all files in `read-papers/`. Gemini drafts the executive summary. Claude Lead reviews the draft, runs the optional NLM sanity check query, and finalizes.
   305	
   306	A 2–3 page summary for decision-makers.
   307	
   308	Every claim and recommendation must explicitly cite sources using the paper's full name. Do not use abbreviations like "the PoLL paper". Use the format: `"Author et al., full paper title, conference year"`.
   309	
   310	The executive summary must include a "Most Fundamental Methods" section describing the top 5 methods from the finalized `method-tracker`.
   311	
   312	The executive summary must include an **"Implications for [Use Case]"** section (or **"Recommendations"** if the Project Context describes a specific project). This section directly answers the key questions or decision points stated in the `## Project Context` section of `README.md`, citing specific papers. For a general academic survey, this section summarizes the most actionable takeaways for the stated audience.
   313	
   314	**Optional NLM sanity check:** After drafting `executive-summary.md`, query NLM with no `source_ids` filter: "Summarize the state of the field based on all sources in this notebook." Compare against the draft and flag any notable discrepancies.
   315	
   316	#### File 4: `method-tracker.md`
   317	
   318	Gradually filled during Phase 3, finalized in Phase 3.5.
   319	
   320	#### Phase 5 Exit Condition: Coverage Evaluation
   321	
   322	Full coverage-evaluation procedure (keyword coverage % + Project Context fitness check + actions when below 95%): see `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § Phase 5 Exit Condition.
   323	
   324	## Final: Append to log.md
   325	
   326	After Phase 5 completes (whether final completion or paused for user input), prepend a dated entry (newest on top) to `[topic-slug]/log.md`. Entry format and rules: see `kb/context/literature-survey/literature-survey-conventions.md` § Final Append to log.md. Use `<skill-name>` = `literature-survey-nlm` for the run header. The log entry should also include `NLM notebook source count: <N> (added <delta> this run)`.

     9	# Literature Survey — Output File Templates
    10	
    11	Shared by `literature-survey` and `literature-survey-nlm`. Skills reference this document; they do not re-state templates inline.
    12	
    13	The four files produced under `[paper-reading-repo]/literature-survey/[topic-slug]/`:

   151	## Method Tracker File Template (`method-tracker.md`)
   152	
   153	```markdown
   154	Date: YYYY-MM-DD (last updated)
   155	Topic: [topic]
   156	
   157	# [topic] - Methodology Fundamentality Tracking
   158	
   159	This file is accumulated automatically while reading PDFs during Phase 3 batch processing.
   160	Update after each batch. Final sorting happens in Phase 3.5.
   161	
   162	## Methodology Table
   163	
   164	| Method Name | Proposal Paper (Year) | Baseline Mention Count | Derived Variant Count | Independent Measured Performance (Dataset: metric | source) | Component Count | Simplicity Score (1-5) | Performance Consistency Score (1-5) | Fundamentality Composite Score |
   165	|---------|----------------|-------------------|----------------|--------------------------------------|-------------|-----------------|----------------------|----------------|
   166	| [MethodA] | [Author et al., year] | 8 | 5 | ImageNet: 82.3% (Paper X), 82.1% (Paper Y) | 2 | 5 | 5 | 38 |
   167	| [MethodB] | [Author et al., year] | 5 | 2 | ... | 3 | 4 | 4 | 22 |
   168	| ... | | | | | | | | |
   169	
   170	## How to Compute the Fundamentality Composite Score
   171	
   172	Composite score = (baseline mention count × 3) + (derived variant count × 2) + (simplicity score × 1) + (performance consistency score × 2)
   173	
   174	- baseline mention count: in how many other papers this method was used as a comparison baseline
   175	- derived variant count: number of papers that directly modified/extended this method
   176	  (mentions such as "based on X", "variant of X" in Related Work)
   177	- simplicity score: 5 = 1–2 components, 4 = 3 components, 3 = 4 components, 2 = 5 components, 1 = 6+ components
   178	- performance consistency score: higher when reported numbers across independent papers have lower variance
   179	  (5 = stddev < 0.5%, 1 = > 3%)
   180	
   181	## Top Method Analysis (Written in Phase 3.5)
   182	
   183	After Phase 3 completes, for the top 10 methods by composite score:
   184	
   185	### Rank 1: [Method Name] (Composite Score: N)
   186	
   187	- Why fundamental: [one-line explanation]
   188	- Representative paper: [author et al., title, conference year]
   189	- Which papers used this method as a baseline: [list]
   190	- Known variants: [list]
   191	- Independent measured performance range: [min] ~ [max] (N papers)
   192	
   193	...
   194	```
   195

   199	Date: YYYY-MM-DD

   215	3. [finding 3]
   216

   218	
   219	This section summarizes the top 5 methods from `method-tracker.md` by composite score.
   220	Criteria for "fundamental":
   221	(1) adopted as a baseline by many papers,
   222	(2) many variants derived from it,
   223	(3) performance is consistent in independent measurements,
   224	(4) structure is simple.
   225	
   226	1. [Method Name] (Composite Score N): [one-line reason it is fundamental]. Proposed in: author et al., full paper title, conference year.
   227	2. [Method Name] (Composite Score N): [one-line reason it is fundamental]. Proposed in: author et al., full paper title, conference year.
   228	3. [Method Name] (Composite Score N): ...
   229	4. ...
   230	5. ...
   231	
   232	## Approach Comparison
   233	
   234	| Approach | Pros | Cons | Related Paper |
   235	|--------|------|------|-----------|
   236	| [Approach 1] | [pros] | [cons] | author et al., [full paper title], [conference] [year] |
   237	| [Approach 2] | [pros] | [cons] | author et al., [full paper title], [conference] [year] |
   238	
   239	## Implications for [Use Case]
   240	
   241	[Read the ## Project Context section from README.md. Write 2–3 paragraphs directly answering the project's key questions or decision points, citing specific papers.
   242	For a general academic survey, summarize the most actionable takeaways for the stated audience.
   243	For a project-specific survey, frame recommendations around the project's practical constraints and goals.]
   244	
   245	## Next Steps
   246	
   247	- [ ] [action item 1]
   248	- [ ] [action item 2]
   249	
   250	## Search Scope
   251	
   252	- Survey period: [date range]
   253	- Total papers: [number]
   254	- Major conferences: [list]
   255	- Search keywords: [list]
   256	```

     1	# 20260917_survey_grok — Follow-up survey: latest industry attribution, and attribution-based retention
     2	
     3	Date: 2026-09-17
     4	Lead: Cursor Grok 4.6 (independent of `../20260917_survey/`)
     5	Parent survey: `../` (attribution-based-retention, Run 1–2, April 2026, 62 papers)
     6	NotebookLM notebook (shared with parent survey; all new sources go here): `6a3b8a8e-6f2f-4efe-99eb-283983fc95d9`
     7	Related notebooks: `unified-ltv-ranking-dating` (`67046a44-7490-4fe5-b54a-3f39ef37fdd3`), `two-sided-market-balancing-dating` (`d3071ac8-16ef-4460-8991-7701679974c8`)
     8	
     9	## Questions this run must answer
    10	
    11	- **Q1.** What is the latest attribution-based approach in the industry? Scope: production or industry-authored attribution systems dated 2025-01 to 2026-09, plus the newest work from the teams and authors behind the classic papers in the parent survey.
    12	- **Q2.** Has anyone in industry published attribution-based retention — that is, attributing user retention, engagement, or days-active to individual in-app interactions (recommendations, matches, messages, notifications, swipes)? No date limit for Q2. Academic work counts if it names a production dataset or deployment.
    13	- **Deliverable.** A recommendation that replaces the current last-touch approach (see Project Context).
    14	
    15	## Project Context
    16	
    17	**Product.** A dating platform. Users swipe on profiles. A mutual like is a match. Matches lead to conversations.
    18	
    19	**Goal.** Attribute a user's retention to particular swipes the user made, so that the platform learns which swipes (and, later, which recommended profiles) keep users coming back. Attribution scores become training labels for a ranking model that predicts, per candidate profile, the incremental retention value of showing it.
    20	
    21	**Current production approach (the baseline to beat).** Last-touch attribution. The N7 retention label of a user (active on day 7 after the reference day) is assigned to the most recent swipes the user made before that. Known weaknesses: it ignores earlier swipes, ignores match and conversation outcomes that occur later, credits swipes that would have happened anyway (selection bias — engaged users swipe more), and gives no fractional credit.
    22	
    23	**Decisions already made (KB, 2026-06-13).** Retention target is binary next-7-day retention over multi-type events (likes, matches, conversations). Two-tier plan: v1 = interpretable removal-effect attributor; v2 = calibrated deep data-driven attribution with a survival head. This run must confirm or revise that plan against what industry now does.
    24	
    25	**What "useful" means for a paper in this run.**
    26	
    27	- It produces per-interaction (per-touch) credit, or a per-action incremental value, not only an aggregate lift.
    28	- It handles a binary or time-to-event outcome that recurs (days active), not only one purchase.
    29	- It handles selection bias: engaged users get more interactions naturally.
    30	- It is deployed, or evaluated on production data, at a consumer platform.
    31	- It is from 2025–2026, or it is from a team the parent survey identified as authoritative.
    32	
    33	**Analogies to map.** Ad touchpoint → swipe or match. Conversion → N7 retention (recurring). Attribution window → lookback window per interaction type. Channel → interaction type (like, match, conversation, notification).
    34	
    35	## Follow list — teams and authors behind the classic papers

    16	* **Method Family:** **LLM-Touchpoint** (LLM-driven semantic reasoning selector using pairwise/listwise prompting over multimodal product/behavior metadata) [5-7].
    17	* **Outcome Being Attributed:** **E-commerce Conversions / Purchases (CVR)** [1, 3, 8].
    18	* **Experiment-Calibrated:** **No** (LLM-selected touchpoint labels are used as auxiliary positive signals to train an industrial multi-task CVR prediction model) [1, 8].

    26	* **Method Family:** **DDA / Bayesian Regression Anchored in MMM** (Temporal disaggregation using adstock transformations + channel-specific PyMC Bayesian linear regression anchored to MMM priors) [12-15].
    27	* **Outcome Being Attributed:** **Daily Campaign-Level Incremental Sales / Revenue** [12, 13, 16].

    36	* **Method Family:** **Front-Door Causal Attribution** (Front-door identification criterion + Adversarial Proxy Mediator + Contrastive InfoNCE overlap control + Inverse Propensity Weighting) [19, 21-23].
    37	* **Outcome Being Attributed:** **Content Creation / User Uploads** (attributing user content creation to prior video consumption touchpoints in a "Consumption-Drives-Production" / CDP setting; *not retention*) [19, 24, 25].

    39	* **User-Level Per-Touch Credit:** **Yes** (computes individualized counterfactual removal uplift \\(\Delta(\tau_j \mid X, T) = g_\theta(X, T) - g_\theta(X, T \setminus \{\tau_j\})\\) for each consumed item in a user's sequence) [22, 25, 29].

     1	# NLM Phase 4-A — q3-last-touch-replacement
     2	
     3	Scoped to this-run source_ids (G1–G25). returncode=0 elapsed=156.7s stdout_bytes=69660
     4	
     5	### **Candidate Replacement Attribution Architectures for Dating App N7 Retention**
     6	
     7	---
     8	
     9	#### **1. Counterfactual Removal-Effect Attribution (ALM-MTA)**
    10	* **Source Paper:** *ALM-MTA: Front-Door Causal Multi-Touch Attribution Method for Creator-Ecosystem Optimization* (Liu et al., Kuaishou Technology, ICLR 2026) [1].
    11	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:** The individualized counterfactual removal drop / uplift:
    12	  \\[\Delta(\tau_j \mid X, T) = g_\theta(X, T) - g_\theta(X, T \setminus \{\tau_j\})\\]
    13	  which measures the exact drop in predicted conversion probability when touchpoint \\(\tau_j\\) is deleted from sequence \\(T\\), identified via front-door adjustment, an adversarially learned proxy mediator \\(M\\), InfoNCE contrastive overlap control, and Inverse Propensity Weighting (IPW) [2-5].
    14	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:** Individual profile swipes, mutual match events, and sent/received messages act as sequential touchpoints \\(\tau_j\\) in a user's multi-touch journey \\(T = \langle \tau_1, \dots, \tau_L \rangle\\) [2]. The model outputs fractional uplift scores per swipe, match, or chat action [2, 4].
    15	* **What It Does NOT Estimate:**
    16	  * It does **not** estimate N7 retention or active days; its target outcome \\(Y \in \{0, 1\}\\) is content creation/upload in a "Consumption-Drives-Production" (CDP) setting [2, 6].
    17	  * It does **not** rely on backdoor adjustment alone, as system-level confounding is unobserved [3, 6].
    18	
    19	---
    20	
    21	#### **2. Experiment-Calibrated Data-Driven Attribution (LinkedIn LiDDA)**
    22	* **Source Paper:** *Buyer Journey Insights with Data-Driven Attribution* / *LiDDA: Data Driven Attribution at LinkedIn* (Bencina et al., LinkedIn, 2025) [7, 8].
    23	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:** Normalized Transformer self-attention weights \\(\alpha_j \in [7]\\) (where \\(\sum \alpha_j = 1.0\\)) from a sequence model \\(P(C \mid EM, EC, S)\\), post-calibrated against controlled marketing A/B holdout experiments and Marketing Mix Models (MMM) [4–5, 9].
    24	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    25	  * **Swipes:** Top-of-funnel discovery touchpoints (\\(p_0\\)) [7].
    26	  * **Matches:** Mid-funnel intent touchpoints [7].
    27	  * **Conversations:** Bottom-of-funnel conversion touchpoints [7].
    28	  * Attention layers allocate fractional credit across the touchpoint sequence \\(S\\) preceding a retention event [9-11].
    29	* **What It Does NOT Estimate:**
    30	  * It does **not** estimate a causal counterfactual removal effect (\\(\Delta \text{N7}\\) if swipe \\(i\\) is removed); it estimates normalized attention shares calibrated against aggregate experiment/MMM priors [9, 12].
    31	  * It does **not** attribute user retention directly (it attributes B2B/B2C marketing lead and sales conversions) [7, 9, 11].
    32	
    33	---
    34	
    35	#### **3. Progressive Feedback & Survival/Time-to-Event (Spotify Impatient Bandits & Kuaishou AURO)**
    36	* **Source Papers:**
    37	  1. *Impatient Bandits: Optimizing for the Long-Term Without Delay* (Zhang et al., Spotify / Imperial College London, JMLR 2026) [13].
    38	  2. *AURO: Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive User Retention Optimization in Recommender Systems* (Xue et al., Kuaishou / NTU, WWW 2025) [14, 15].
    39	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:**
    40	  * **Impatient Bandits:** Gaussian posterior expectation of an item's 60-day cumulative active days \\(\theta_a \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{t,a}, \Sigma_{t,a})\\), updated online as daily progressive active-day feedback vectors \\(Y_u^{(j)} \in \{0, 1\}\\) arrive over 60 days [13].
    41	  * **AURO:** Delayed episode-level user return-time hazard/reward \\(r_t = \lambda \times \text{user return time}\\), propagated via a value-based state abstraction network \\(\phi(s_t)\\) and guarded online exploration [3, 17, 27–30].
    42	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    43	  * **Impatient Bandits:** A profile card / match recommendation is action \\(a\\); daily user app returns post-match form the progressive daily feedback trace \\(Y_u^{(1)}, Y_u^{(2)}, \dots, Y_u^{(60)}\\) [13].
    44	  * **AURO:** The swiping/messaging session state is \\(s_t\\), ranking weights are action \\(a_t\\), and the time elapsed until the user's next app open is the episode-ending reward \\(r_t\\) [15, 18–19].
    45	* **What They Do NOT Estimate:**
    46	  * They do **not** produce a multi-touch sequence attribution table across historical swipes/matches; they estimate item/policy-level active-day yield or return-time distributions for online ranking/bandits [13].
    47	
    48	---
    49	
    50	#### **4. Surrogate Save-Revisit & Downstream Rewards (Pinterest Downstream Rewards & Save-Revisit)**
    51	* **Source Papers:**
    52	  1. *Long-term User Engagement Optimization through Model-agnostic Downstream Rewards Learning* (Wang et al., Pinterest, 2026) [16, 17].
    53	  2. *Save, Revisit, Retain: A Scalable Framework for Enhancing User Retention in Large-Scale Recommender Systems* (Jiang et al., Pinterest, AAAI 2026) [18].
    54	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:**
    55	  * **Downstream Rewards:** Expected downstream session trajectory rewards \\(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{u,t} = \mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{E}}[\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\mathbf{S}}}[\mathbb{R}(\hat{\mathbf{S}})]]\\) combining deeper session engagement (P2P rabbit hole exploration), shallow closeup penalties (\\(<1\text{s}\\) duration), and use-case adoption (\\(\mathbb{R}^{\text{UCA}}\\)) [38–41].
    56	  * **Save-Revisit:** Joint probability of a candidate item being saved (repinned) and subsequently revisited via own-profile impressions or grid-clicks within 0–6 days (\\(1\text{dRevImpre}, 1\text{dRevGrid}, 7\text{dRevGrid}\\)) [113–115].
    57	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    58	  * **Candidate Profile Card:** Source item \\(x_{u,t}\\) [19].
    59	  * **Right-Swipe / Match:** Equivalent to a "Save" / Repin [18, 20].
    60	  * **Deep Conversation:** Equivalent to entering a deep "P2P rabbit hole" or grid-click revisit [17, 21].
    61	  * **Shallow Right-Swipe:** Right-swiping or viewing a profile for \\(<1\text{s}\\) without sending a message maps to the shallow closeup negative penalty [22].
    62	* **What They Do NOT Estimate:**
    63	  * They do **not** estimate causal removal attribution (MTA) or fractional credit decomposition of individual past swipes on N7 retention [17, 18]. They estimate predictive surrogate reward heads for multi-task rankers [19, 23].
    64	
    65	---
    66	
    67	#### **5. Interpretable Per-Item Retention Scores (IURO / IURO+)**
    68	* **Source Papers:** *Interpretable User Retention-Oriented Optimization* (Ding et al., Zhihu / WeChat / Tencent, RecSys 2023 [24] & IURO+ ACM TOIS 2026 [25]).
    69	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:** Item-level UI retention scores \\(s_{ui}\\) generated by a UI Scorer module via Behavior-wise Contrastive Multi-Instance Learning (BCMIL) with instance-level attention \\(y_{\text{MIL}} = \sum \text{softmax}(\cdot) s_{ui}\\) and contrastive "aha item" margin loss \\(L_{\text{CL}}\\) [184–187, 194, 199–200].
    70	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    71	  * Past swipes, matches, and messaging sessions form the short-term historical interaction sequence (instance bag) [26, 27].
    72	  * High-retention profiles that resulted in deep conversations are identified as "aha items" and assigned high retention weights \\(s_{ui}\\) [24, 28].
    73	* **What It Does NOT Estimate:**
    74	  * It does **not** estimate an identified causal counterfactual removal contrast (\\(\Delta \text{N7}\\) if swipe \\(i\\) is removed) [28, 29]. It uses contrastive multi-instance attention to sharpen retention-correlated "aha items" [28, 30].
    75	
    76	---
    77	
    78	#### **6. Sleeping Bandits & Recency Decay (Duolingo Recovering Difference Softmax)**
    79	* **Source Paper:** *A Sleeping, Recovering Bandit Algorithm for Optimizing Recurring Notifications* (Yancey & Settles, Duolingo, KDD 2020) [31].
    80	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:** Inverse propensity-weighted (IPW) relative difference score \\(\hat{s}_a = \frac{\hat{\mu}_a^+ - \hat{\mu}_a^-}{\hat{\mu}_a^-}\\) comparing eligible-and-sent vs. eligible-and-not-sent rounds, regularized via empirical Bayes shrinkage and penalized by an exponential recency decay \\(-\gamma \cdot 2^{-d_{a,t}/h}\\) [228–230].
    81	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    82	  * **Re-engagement Push / Match Reminder / Profile Card:** Arm \\(a\\) [31].
    83	  * **Sleeping Arms (Eligibility):** Profiles or push templates available only under specific user conditions (e.g., active match pending) [32].
    84	  * **2-Hour App Open / Message Sent:** Immediate binary reward \\(r_t \in \{0, 1\}\\) [33, 34].
    85	  * **Recency Decay:** Reduces the score of recently shown profiles or notification templates to prevent fatigue [35].
    86	* **What It Does NOT Estimate:**
    87	  * It does **not** estimate multi-touch sequence attribution across past swipes/matches, nor long-term N7 retention directly (evaluates a short 2-hour window) [33, 34].
    88	
    89	---
    90	
    91	#### **7. Item-Wise Future-Impact Decomposition & Direct Causal Effect Optimization (ItemA2C & DCEO)**
    92	* **Source Papers:**
    93	  1. *ItemA2C: Future Impact Decomposition in Request-level Recommendations* (Wang et al., Kuaishou, KDD 2024) [36, 37].
    94	  2. *DCEO: Direct Causal Effect Optimization for Long-Term User Value Modeling in E-commerce Search* (Zhang et al., Alibaba / Taobao, 2026) [38, 39].
    95	* **Exact Credit Object Estimated:**
    96	  * **ItemA2C:** Item-decomposed advantage function \\(A(s_t, i_{t,k}, w_{t,k}) = \Psi_w(s_t, i_{t,k}) - \frac{V(s_t)}{K}\\), allocating slate-level future value \\(V(s_{t+1})\\) to individual items via reward-based or model-based adversarial weights \\(w_{t,k}\\) [40-42].
    97	  * **DCEO:** Relative Causal Effect (RCE) of an aggregated, calibrated user-level proxy metric \\(P_u(\theta)\\) on \\(n\\)-day cumulative user value \\(Y_u\\), optimized via an actor-critic model under a 5% relative proxy intervention [39, 43-45].
    98	* **Mapping onto Swipe / Match / Conversation:**
    99	  * **Swipe Deck:** Request slate \\(a_t\\) [46].
   100	  * **Profile Card:** Individual item \\(i_{t,k}\\) [40].
   101	  * **Right-Swipe / Match / Conversation:** Item-wise immediate rewards \\(r_{t,k}\\) [40].
   102	  * **DCEO Context Weights:** Upstream predictions (\\(p\text{Swipe}, p\text{Match}, p\text{Chat}\\)) map to \\(v_{uri}^m\\), and actor context weights \\(w_{ur}^m\\) combine them into an item proxy score [44, 47].
   103	* **What They Do NOT Estimate:**
   104	  * They do **not** estimate post-hoc multi-touch attribution of N7 retention back to past swipes outside the live ranking policy [37, 40, 44].
   105	
   106	---
   107	
   108	### **Methodological Summary & Incremental Contrast Flag**
   109	
   110	> ⚠️ **Flag regarding Incremental Removal Contrast on N7 Retention:**
   111	> **None of the scoped papers estimate the true incremental effect of removing one swipe (or one item) on binary N7 retention.**
   112	> 
   113	> * **ALM-MTA** (Kuaishou 2026) is the **only** paper that estimates the exact counterfactual removal effect (\\(\Delta(\tau_j \mid X, T) = g_\theta(X, T) - g_\theta(X, T \setminus \{\tau_j\})\\)) of deleting a single touchpoint from a user sequence [2, 4]; however, its outcome is **content creation/upload (\\(Y\\))**, *not* N7 retention [2, 6].
   114	> * **DCEO** (Alibaba 2026) estimates the causal effect of a 5% relative increase in an aggregated user-level proxy metric on \\(n\\)-day cumulative GMV/purchases, *not* a per-swipe removal contrast [39, 43, 44].
   115	> * All other candidate frameworks estimate predictive correlation, Transformer attention shares (LiDDA) [9, 10], RL advantage decomposition (ItemA2C) [40, 42], instance-level attention sharpening (IURO) [26, 28], or surrogate reward proxies (Pinterest, Duolingo, Spotify) [13, 17, 23, 33]. None publish an identified causal removal contrast on N7 retention.

     1	# NLM Phase 4-A — q4-bias-eval
     2	
     3	Scoped to this-run source_ids (G1–G25). returncode=0 elapsed=149.6s stdout_bytes=111628
     4	
     5	### **Catalog of Selection-Bias & Confounding Devices**
     6	
     7	#### **1. Front-Door Identification & Adversarial Proxy Mediation**
     8	* **Paper / System Source:** *ALM-MTA: Front-Door Causal Multi-Touch Attribution Method for Creator-Ecosystem Optimization* (Kuaishou Technology, ICLR 2026) [1, 2].
     9	* **Bias Addressed:** Resolves unobserved system-level and sequence-level confounding (e.g., latent user intent, unmeasured platform recommendation strategies, and social influences) that invalidate backdoor adjustments in observational logs [2, 3].
    10	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** It does not identify causal effects if the latent mediator (\\(M\\)) fails path containment (i.e., if direct touchpoint-to-outcome \\(T \to Y\\) paths exist that bypass \\(M\\)) or if the adversarial proxy (\\(Y'\\)) leaks direct outcome information [2, 4, 5]. Furthermore, its target outcome is creator content upload (\\(Y\\)), *not* user retention [6, 7].
    11	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:** **YES.** Outputs individualized counterfactual removal uplift labels \\(\Delta(\tau_j \mid X, T) = g_\theta(X, T) - g_\theta(X, T \setminus \{\tau_j\})\\) for each consumed touchpoint \\(\tau_j\\) in a user sequence [180, 189–190].
    12	
    13	---
    14	
    15	#### **2. Experiment-Calibrated Data-Driven Attribution (DDA) & MMM Anchoring**
    16	* **Paper / System Sources:** 
    17	  1. *Buyer Journey Insights with Data-Driven Attribution* / *LiDDA* (LinkedIn, 2025) [3–4].
    18	  2. *Integrated Marketing Attribution (IMA)* (Lowe's Companies, Inc., 2026) [8, 9].
    19	* **Bias Addressed:** Over-indexing on low-funnel conversion channels (e.g., Search or Email) inherent in last-touch rules [10, 11]. Resolves missing member-level paid media impression data via probabilistic imputation [5–6]. IMA addresses campaign-level high-dimensionality and cross-campaign multicollinearity by anchoring daily campaign models to macro-level Marketing Mix Modeling (MMM) priors [9, 12].
    20	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** It does not identify item-level counterfactual removal effects (\\(\Delta \text{Outcome}\\) if a single touchpoint is deleted) [13, 14]. LiDDA outputs normalized self-attention weights calibrated to aggregate holdout experiment/MMM ratios [13, 15], while IMA operates on aggregated daily campaign media data rather than user-level touchpoints [8, 12].
    21	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:**
    22	  * **LiDDA:** **YES** (member-level per-touchpoint normalized attention weights) [13, 16].
    23	  * **IMA:** **NO** (campaign-level daily sales disaggregation, no user-level touchpoint credit) [8, 12].
    24	
    25	---
    26	
    27	#### **3. Always-On Holdbacks & Dual-Threshold Causal Targeting (Deep Twin Network)**
    28	* **Paper / System Source:** *Incremental Recommendation via Causal Models* (Spotify, 2026) [254–255].
    29	* **Bias Addressed:** Selection bias toward "always-takers"—users with high organic affinity who would discover or stream content regardless of a recommendation [17, 18]. Resolves attribution window mismatches (short direct-response window for treated \\(Y(1)\\) vs. multi-day organic window for holdback \\(Y(0)\\)) [17, 19].
    30	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** It does not estimate a valid point-wise CATE subtraction \\(p_1(\mathbf{x}) - p_0(\mathbf{x})\\) directly due to the temporal window mismatch [18, 20], nor does it produce fractional multi-touch credit across past user interaction sequences [18, 20].
    31	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:** **NO.** It produces dual propensity scores (\\(p_1(\mathbf{x})\\) for recommendation stream probability and \\(p_0(\mathbf{x})\\) for organic stream probability) to filter non-incremental impressions via a dual-threshold policy \\(\mathbb{1}[p_1(\mathbf{x}) \ge \theta_1] \cdot \mathbb{1}[p_0(\mathbf{x}) \le \theta_0]\\) at serving time [18, 20].
    32	
    33	---
    34	
    35	#### **4. Interleaving (Competitive Pair Team Drafting) & Online Counterfactual Evaluation**
    36	* **Paper / System Source:** *Harnessing the Power of Interleaving and Counterfactual Evaluation for Airbnb Search Ranking* (Airbnb, KDD 2025) [21, 22].
    37	* **Bias Addressed:** Position bias, long observation delays, and low statistical power in standard conversion-based A/B testing (accelerating online evaluation velocity up to 100x) [21, 23, 24].
    38	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** Interleaving breaks down when rankers perform set-level optimization (e.g., secondary objective re-ranking or diversity), as side-by-side presentation distorts user choice [25-27]. Counterfactual evaluation depends on position-decay assumptions (\\(\gamma^{-r}\\)) and does not yield historical multi-touch credit for user retention [135–136, 141].
    39	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:** **NO.** Attributes booking preferences to listing search appearances to evaluate ranking policy variants before A/B deployment; does not publish persistent per-touch sequence credit tables [28, 29].
    40	
    41	---
    42	
    43	#### **5. Sleeping & Recovering Bandits (IPW Relative Scoring & Recency Decay)**
    44	* **Paper / System Source:** *A Sleeping, Recovering Bandit Algorithm for Optimizing Recurring Notifications* (Duolingo, KDD 2020) [30, 31].
    45	* **Bias Addressed:** Conditional eligibility bias (sleeping arms—where template availability is confounded by user activity/state) [30, 32] and user desensitization/fatigue (recovering arms—where novelty wears off) [30, 33].
    46	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** It does not identify multi-touch sequence attribution across past user interactions [298–299], nor long-term multi-day retention directly (reward is evaluated strictly in a 2-hour post-notification window) [34].
    47	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:** **NO.** It computes arm-level relative difference scores \\(\hat{s}_a = \frac{\hat{\mu}_a^+ - \hat{\mu}_a^-}{\hat{\mu}_a^-}\\) with empirical Bayes shrinkage and recency decay penalties (\\(2^{-d_{a,t}/h}\\)) for Softmax template selection [33, 35, 36].
    48	
    49	---
    50	
    51	#### **6. Observational vs. RCT Gaps & Propensity-Stratified Evaluation Protocols**
    52	* **Paper / System Sources:**
    53	  1. *ALM-MTA* (Kuaishou, ICLR 2026) [1, 37].
    54	  2. *Buyer Journey Insights* (LinkedIn, 2025) [13, 14].
    55	  3. *Impatient Bandits* (Spotify, JMLR 2026) [38, 39].
    56	  4. *AURO* (Kuaishou, WWW 2025) [40, 41].
    57	* **Bias Addressed:** Off-policy distribution shift, spurious correlations in historical logs, and evaluation bias when true randomized controlled trial (RCT) labels are unavailable [37, 39, 42].
    58	* **Protocol Details:** ALM-MTA introduces a non-personalized, propensity-stratified grouped AUUC protocol using Shapley value sampling across \\(K \approx 1000\\) treatment clusters [224–226]. Spotify Impatient Bandits uses Type II Maximum Likelihood prior fitting and Value of Progressive Feedback (VoPF) information bounds [39, 43, 44]. LinkedIn evaluates channel lift counterfactuals against holdout A/B tests [14].
    59	* **What It Does NOT Identify:** Propensity stratification and off-policy estimators (NCIS, IPW) control for observed covariates \\(X\\), but fail if unobserved confounding \\(W\\) violates unconfoundedness (unless front-door mechanisms are applied) [3, 42].
    60	* **Yields Per-Interaction Credit?:** **Varies by method.** The protocol itself is an evaluation/debiasing procedure; ALM-MTA produces per-touch credit [6, 45], whereas RL/bandit evaluation protocols do not [46, 47].
    61	
    62	---
    63	
    64	### **Negative Evidence: Systems That Optimize Retention Without Producing Per-Touch Credit**
    65	
    66	The following systems from the scoped sources optimize retention, revisitation, or multi-day engagement via RL, learning-to-rank, bandits, or surrogate rewards, but **do NOT produce or publish a per-touch sequence attribution credit table**:
    67	
    68	1. **AURO (Adaptive User Retention Optimization)** (*Kuaishou Technology / NTU, WWW 2025*): Hidden Parameter MDP (HiP-MDP) with value-based state abstraction loss \\(J(\phi)\\) and guarded online exploration [40, 46, 48]. Optimizes delayed user return time rewards (\\(r_t = \lambda \times \text{return time}\\)), outputting continuous action vectors for candidate ranking policy execution without per-touch attribution [46, 48].
    69	2. **ItemA2C (Future Impact Decomposition)** (*Kuaishou Technology, KDD 2024*): Request-level MDP with item-decomposed advantage actor-critic [49, 50]. Decomposes list-level future value \\(V(s_{t+1})\\) into item-level targets \\(\Psi_w(s_t, i_{t,k})\\) via adversarial/reward-based weights \\(w_{t,k}\\) to update ranking policy parameters \\(\pi_\theta\\), without generating a reusable attribution credit table [50-52].
    70	3. **GFN4Retention (Generative Flow Networks for Retention)** (*Kuaishou Technology, KDD 2024*): Probabilistic flow-matching network propagating end-of-session retention reward \\(R\\) across recommendation steps via detailed balance loss [53-55]. Generates candidate list selection policies without per-item sequence attribution labels [56, 57].
    71	4. **Pinterest Downstream Rewards & Save-Revisit Frameworks** (*Pinterest, 2026 / AAAI 2026*): Offline screening under User-Prevalence (UP) normalization identifies session-level surrogates (P2P rabbit hole exploration, shallow closeup penalties, use-case adoption, 0–6 day save-gridclicks) [58-61]. Multi-task prediction heads are combined via HyperOPT out-of-graph weights \\(s(x) = \sum w_i p_i(x)\\) for candidate rankers; no per-interaction credit table is produced [58, 62, 63].
    72	5. **Google / YouTube Reward Surrogates & Learned Ranking Function (LRF)** (*Google Research, KDD 2022 / RecSys 2024*): Modulates REINFORCE rewards via 2-week entropy diversity multipliers [64, 65] or computes uplift over abandonment \\(\frac{p_{\text{clk}}}{p_{\text{clk}}+p_{\text{abd}}} (R_{\text{clk}} - R_{\text{abd}})\\) to output candidate slate scores [66-68]; does not produce multi-touch sequence attribution tables [64, 67].
    73	6. **Spotify Impatient Bandits** (*Spotify / Imperial College London, JMLR 2026*): Gaussian Bayesian filter updating 60-day active-day posterior stickiness beliefs \\(\theta_a\\) as daily progressive engagement signals arrive [38, 39, 69]. Used for Thompson Sampling podcast show recommendations without multi-touch historical sequence credit [47, 69, 70].
    74	7. **RLUR (Reinforcing User Retention)** (*Kuaishou Technology, WWW 2023*): Infinite-horizon DDPG critic \\(Q_T(s,a)\\) minimizing cumulative return time gap between app sessions [71, 72]. Outputs ranking policy parameters without touchpoint attribution [71, 73].
    75	8. **DT4Rec & DT4IER (Decision Transformer for Retention)** (*Baidu / Academic, WWW 2023 / 2024*): Autoregressive sequence generation prompted by discretized Return-to-Go (RTG) vectors [74-76]. Outputs recommended item sequences rather than per-touch attribution credit tables [74, 75].
    76	9. **DCEO (Direct Causal Effect Optimization)** (*Alibaba / Taobao, 2026*): Actor model \\(f_\theta\\) predicts context-dependent fusion weights \\(w_{ur}^m\\) over upstream scores, aggregated and calibrated (\\(g_\psi\\)) to a reference impression count (\\(C=100\\)) to optimize Relative Causal Effect on 4-day GMV via critic \\(h_\phi\\) [77-79]. Produces ranking fusion weights, not a per-touch attribution table [78, 79].
    77	10. **Matching for Retention (MRet)** (*Academic / Dating Platform Research, 2026*): Evaluates personalized retention curves \\(f(x, m_{1:\tau})\\) to maximize mutual user retention probabilities in two-sided matching, operating as a learning-to-rank allocation algorithm without fractional touchpoint credit [80-82].

     1	# D3 — Q2 Discovery: Attribution-Based Retention (Per-Touch Credit)
     2	
     3	**Discovery date:** 2026-09-17  
     4	**Agent:** grok discovery pass (D3)  
     5	**Q2 definition:** Does the method assign **per-interaction credit** for delayed retention / days-active / N-day return to a **specific in-app action or exposure** (MTA-style), vs merely **optimizing a ranking/RL policy** for retention?
     6	
     7	**Classification key**
     8	| Label | Meaning |
     9	|-------|---------|
    10	| **TRUE (MTA-style)** | Publishes per-touch credit of retention/active-days to individual actions/exposures; causal or explicit multi-touch decomposition |
    11	| **PARTIAL** | Item/action-level retention scores or surrogate linkage, but no identified causal MTA / confounding acknowledged |
    12	| **NOT attribution** | RL, bandit, LTR, or surrogate-reward **policy optimization**; no per-exposure retention credit table |
    13	
    14	---
    15	
    16	## Executive summary
    17	
    18	Published **strict** per-touch retention attribution (identified causal credit of N-day active to one swipe/match/rec item amid many exposures) was **not found** in industry or dating-specific literature. The closest production-adjacent hits are **partial**: Pinterest save→revisit linkage, Tencent WeChat KanYiKan **IURO** UI retention scores per historical item, and Kuaishou **FID** item-wise future-impact decomposition. The bulk of “retention” literature is **RL / ranking optimization** (RLUR, GFN4Retention, AURO, LRF, etc.) — near-misses, not attribution.
    19	
    20	**Count (this discovery pass):** **3 partial per-touch retention-credit hits** (RevisitMTL, IURO, FID) + **1 touchpoint-incrementality hit** (Netflix message holdback, notification not in-app item) vs **~18 RL/ranking retention-optimization near-misses** logged below.
    21	
    22	---
    23	
    24	## A. Partial per-interaction retention credit (closest Q2 hits)
    25	
    26	### A1. Save, Revisit, Retain (RevisitMTL)
    27	
    28	| Field | Value |
    29	|-------|-------|
    30	| **Classification** | **PARTIAL** — deterministic surrogate attribution: save on Pin *i* → profile revisitation within 0–7 days; not full-path MTA, confounders acknowledged |
    31	| **Company / dataset** | Pinterest Inc.; Related Pins / Search, 500M+ users; 24M-user online A/B |
    32	| **Outcome** | Active users (+0.10% A/B); same-day / 1-day / 7-day profile revisitation labels |
    33	| **URL** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18013 |
    34	
    35	Explicitly frames “accurate attribution” of which exposure triggered revisit; uses save→revisit event pipeline as surrogate for causal effect of saving on return. Credits **specific saved Pins**, not all retention paths (notifications, direct open, etc.).
    36	
    37	---
    38	
    39	### A2. Interpretable User Retention Modeling (IURO)
    40	
    41	| Field | Value |
    42	|-------|-------|
    43	| **Classification** | **PARTIAL** — per **user–item interaction** UI retention score via CMIL/RMIL; identifies sparse “Aha items”; not causal MTA |
    44	| **Company / dataset** | Tencent WeChat “KanYiKan” (看一看); ZhihuRec offline + production A/B on WeChat recommendation |
    45	| **Outcome** | N-day user return (retention rate); online A/B reported retention lift (exact % in paper Table 3) |
    46	| **URL** | https://doi.org/10.1145/3604915.3608818 |
    47	
    48	UI scorer assigns a retention score to **each historical user–item interaction**; attention aggregates to find influential items. Combined with online CTR at serving — interpretability-focused, not Shapley/removal-effect attribution.
    49	
    50	Follow-on: *Rethinking User Retention Modeling in Recommendation* (TOIS 2026) — https://doi.org/10.1145/3790098
    51	
    52	---
    53	
    54	### A3. Future Impact Decomposition (FID / ItemA2C)
    55	
    56	| Field | Value |
    57	|-------|-------|
    58	| **Classification** | **PARTIAL** — RL item-wise future-impact decomposition (reward-based + adversarial re-weighting); trajectory credit not causal identification |
    59	| **Company / dataset** | Kuaishou Technology; KuaiRand, MovieLens-1M; Kuaishou online |
    60	| **Outcome** | User retention = probability of return **next week** (+0.016% online); DAU +0.028% |
    61	| **URL** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.16108 |
    62	
    63	Authors: Xiaobei Wang, Shuchang Liu, Qingpeng Cai, Peng Jiang, Kun Gai, et al. Decomposes request-level future value to **per-item** contributions within list — closest Kuaishou mechanism to item-level delayed credit, but still policy-learning not published attribution tables.
    64	
    65	Code: https://github.com/wangxiaobei565/itemdecomposition
    66	
    67	---
    68	
    69	### A4. Netflix message-level holdback (touchpoint incrementality)
    70	
    71	| Field | Value |
    72	|-------|-------|
    73	| **Classification** | **PARTIAL (touchpoint, not in-app item)** — always-on 2% message holdback measures **incremental downstream viewing** per notification/message |
    74	| **Company / dataset** | Netflix; production messaging system |
    75	| **Outcome** | Incremental content viewing (not strictly N-day DAU); contrasts open/click vs incremental watch |
    76	| **URL** | https://medium.com/notificationsblog/incrementality-focused-messaging-measurement-all-the-time-everywhere-94ef0c229368 |
    77	
    78	Per-**message** causal incrementality via randomized withholding — MTA-analog for **notifications**, not per-feed-item or swipe retention credit.
    79

    82	## B. Dating-platform hits (highest value; mostly NOT per-touch attribution)
    83	
    84	### B1. Beyond Match Maximization and Fairness: Retention-Optimized Two-Sided Matching (MRet)
    85	
    86	| Field | Value |
    87	|-------|-------|
    88	| **Classification** | **NOT attribution** — pair-level retention-curve LTR; joint retention gain of viewer + recommended user |
    89	| **Company / dataset** | Undisclosed major online dating platform + synthetic two-sided data |
    90	| **Outcome** | User retention (ecosystem-level); higher retention vs match-max / fairness baselines in sim + real data |
    91	| **URL** | https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15752 |
    92	
    93	No per-swipe/match/conversation credit for N-day active.
    94	
    95	---
    96	
    97	### B2. So, Who Likes You? Evidence from a Randomized Field Experiment
    98	
    99	| Field | Value |
   100	|-------|-------|
   101	| **Classification** | **NOT attribution** — RCT on WLY feature; causal effect on messages/matches, not retention days-active credit |
   102	| **Company / dataset** | Major North American dating platform (unnamed; often inferred Hinge-class) |
   103	| **Outcome** | +7.4% messages (women), +14.4% / +11.5% matches; engagement not N-day retention decomposition |
   104	| **URL** | https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.2022.4576 |
   105	
   106	---
   107	
   108	### B3. The Secret to Finding a Match: Field Experiment on Choice Capacity
   109	
   110	| Field | Value |
   111	|-------|-------|
   112	| **Classification** | **NOT attribution** — RCT on choice capacity → engagement/matching |
   113	| **Company / dataset** | Online dating platform (collaborator unnamed) |
   114	| **Outcome** | Engagement and matching outcomes by gender/capacity arm |
   115	| **URL** | https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=3517018 |
   116	
   117	---
   118	
   119	### B4. Positive Customer Churn: An Application to Online Dating
   120	
   121	| Field | Value |
   122	|-------|-------|
   123	| **Classification** | **NOT attribution** — cause-specific **survival/hazard** model; reciprocal interactions → positive churn (success exit), not per-touch credit |
   124	| **Company / dataset** | German online dating panel (1,369 respondents) |
   125	| **Outcome** | Positive vs negative churn hazards; reciprocal messages increase positive churn (β=1.012) |
   126	| **URL** | https://doi.org/10.1177/1094670518795054 |
   127	
   128	Survival framing adjacent to Q2 but does **not** assign retention credit to individual likes/swipes.
   129	
   130	---
   131

   133	
   134	| Field | Value |
   135	|-------|-------|
   136	| **Classification** | **NOT attribution** — dynamic assortment + causal effect of **past matches on like behavior**; optimizes matches not retention credit |
   137	| **Company / dataset** | Major US online dating company (industry partner) |
   138	| **Outcome** | +27% matches in field experiments; past-match count negatively affects likes |
   139	| **URL** | https://doi.org/10.1287/msom.2022.1107 |

   150	| **URL** | https://doi.org/10.25300/misq/2019/14289 |

   165	## C. Kuaishou author cluster (Qingpeng Cai, Shuchang Liu, Kun Gai, Peng Jiang)
   166	
   167	| Paper | Class | Company / dataset | Outcome | URL |
   168	|-------|-------|-------------------|---------|-----|
   169	| **RLUR** — Reinforcing User Retention in a Billion Scale Short Video RS (WWW 2023) | **NOT attribution** | Kuaishou production; billion-scale logs | Next-day retention, DAU; fully deployed | https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.01724 |
   170	| **GFN4Retention** — Modeling User Retention through GFN (KDD 2024) | **NOT attribution** (flow back-prop is trajectory credit) | Kuaishou + KuaiRand; production A/B | Cross-session return time / retention reward | https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06043 |
   171	| **AURO** — Adaptive User Retention Optimization (WWW 2025) | **NOT attribution** | Kuaishou live short-video (~25M DAU) + MovieLens + simulator | Retention / return-time RL under non-stationarity | https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.03984 |
   172	| **SEC** — Stratified Expert Cloning for Retention-Aware Rec (CIKM 2025) | **NOT attribution** | Kuaishou & Kuaishou Lite 200M+ DAU | Active-days via imitation from high-retention strata | https://arxiv.org/html/2504.05628v2 |
   173	| **FID** — Future Impact Decomposition (KDD 2024) | **PARTIAL** | Kuaishou online | Week-ahead return prob +0.016% | https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.16108 |
   174	| **OCARM** — Distilling Post-Conversion Content for User Retention (arXiv 2026) | **NOT attribution** | Kuaishou RTB re-engagement | LT1/LT7/LT30 prediction at bid level | https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25839 |
   175	
   176	RLUR abstract explicitly states retention is “hard to decompose … to each item or a list of items” — motivation for RL, not MTA.

   270	
   271	## H. Q2 verdict (D3)
   272	
   273	1. **Strict Q2 (identified per-touch causal credit of N-day active to one in-app action):** **Not found** in industry or dating literature searched.
   274	2. **Best partial hits:** Pinterest **RevisitMTL** (save→7d revisit), Tencent **IURO** (UI retention score per historical item), Kuaishou **FID** (item future-impact decomposition).
   275	3. **Dating:** Multiple causal/survival papers on matches/engagement/churn; **zero** published swipe→days-active attribution systems from Tinder/Hinge/Bumble/Match Group.
   276	4. **Dominant pattern:** Retention-aware **RL / LTR / bandit / surrogate reward** (RLUR, GFN4Retention, AURO, LRF, Wang 2022, Duolingo KDD 2020) — optimizes policy, does not publish per-exposure retention credit.
   277	5. **Adjacent incrementality:** Netflix message holdback and Douyin CFR-DF measure **treatment/message-level** incremental effects, not recommender item MTA for retention.
   278

   281	*End D3 discovery.*
